> **User (2025-05-07 19:16:38 UTC):** > > Provide official certificate of indigency **Assistant (2025-05-07 19:16:41 UTC):** To obtain the official **Application for Determination of Civil Indigent Status** required by the Florida courts, you can download it directly from the Florida Courts website: **[Download the Application for Determination of Civil Indigent Status (PDF)](https://www.flcourts.gov/content/download/403010/file/indigent_application.pdf)** --- ### 📄 **Instructions for Completing the Application** 1. **Personal Information:** - Provide your full legal name, date of birth, and the last four digits of your Social Security Number or Driver's License. - Include your current address, phone number, and email address. 2. **Case Information:** - Enter the name of the court handling your case (e.g., Miami-Dade County Circuit Court). - Provide the case number and the names of the plaintiff/petitioner and defendant/respondent. 3. **Financial Information:** - **Dependents:** List the number of dependents you have. - **Income:** Detail your net income, including salary, wages, bonuses, and any other sources of income. - **Other Income:** Indicate any additional income sources such as Social Security benefits, unemployment compensation, pensions, etc. - **Assets:** List any assets you own, including cash, bank accounts, vehicles, real estate, stocks, bonds, etc. - **Liabilities and Debts:** Provide information on your debts, including mortgages, car loans, credit card debts, medical bills, etc. 4. **Legal Representation:** - Indicate whether you have a private attorney representing you in this case. 5. **Certification:** - Read the certification statement carefully. - Sign and date the application, attesting that the information provided is true and accurate. --- ### 📝 **Submitting the Application** - **Where to Submit:** File the completed application with the **Clerk of the Court** in the county where your case is being heard.

For example, if your case is in Miami-Dade County, submit it to the Miami-Dade County Clerk's Office. - **Application Fee:** There may be a nominal application fee (e.g., $50), but this can often be waived if you qualify for indigent status. - **Clerk's Determination:** The clerk will review your application and determine whether you qualify for indigent status.

Let me know if you want these zipped or previewed inline. > **User (2025-05-02 22:47:45 UTC):** > > Do you have all the info about the 3rd circuit court of appeals writ of prohibition **Assistant (2025-05-02 22:47:49 UTC):** Under the Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure (FRAP), Rule 21 governs the procedure for seeking writs of mandamus, prohibition, and other extraordinary writs from a U.S.

These writs are exceptional remedies, typically invoked when no other adequate means exist to attain relief, and are used to compel or prohibit actions by lower courts or officials. --- ### 🏛️ Rule 21 – Writs of Mandamus and Prohibition, and Other Extraordinary Writs #### (a) **Mandamus or Prohibition to a Court: Petition, Filing, Service, and Docketing** 1. **Filing and Service**: A party petitioning for a writ of mandamus or prohibition directed to a court must file the petition with the circuit clerk and serve it on all parties to the proceeding in the trial court.

The party must also provide a copy to the trial-court judge.

All parties to the proceeding in the trial court other than the petitioner are respondents for all purposes. 2. **Petition Requirements**: - **Title**: The petition must be titled “In re [name of petitioner].” - **Contents**: The petition must state: - The relief sought; - The issues presented; - The facts necessary to understand the issue presented by the petition; and - The reasons why the writ should issue. - **Attachments**: The petition must include a copy of any order or opinion or parts of the record that may be essential to understand the matters set forth in the petition. 3. **Docketing**: Upon receiving the prescribed docket fee, the clerk must docket the petition and submit it to the court. #### (b) **Denial; Order Directing Answer; Briefs; Precedence** 1. **Court's Response**: The court may deny the petition without an answer.

Otherwise, it must order the respondent, if any, to answer within a fixed time. 2. **Service of Order**: The clerk must serve the order to respond on all persons directed to respond. 3. **Joint Answers**: Two or more respondents may answer jointly. 4. **Precedence**: The proceeding must be given preference over ordinary civil cases. 5. **Notification**: The circuit clerk must send a copy of the final disposition to the trial-court judge. #### (c) **Other Extraordinary Writs** An application for an extraordinary writ other than one provided for in Rule 21(a) must be made by filing a petition with the circuit clerk and serving it on the respondents.

Proceedings on the application must conform, so far as is practicable, to the procedures prescribed in Rule 21(a) and (b). --- ### ⚖️ Legal Foundation The authority for courts of appeals to issue such extraordinary writs is derived from 28 U.S.C. § 1651, known > **User (2025-05-02 22:48:32 UTC):** > > Do you have all the info about the 3rd circuit court of appeals writ of prohibition against judge Enriquez **Assistant (2025-05-02 22:48:35 UTC):** As of now, there is no publicly available information regarding an Order Denying Rehearing in the case of Cody Enriquez, docket number 4D22-0694, from the Florida Fourth District Court of Appeal.

The **OCR improvement pipeline** can also leverage these algorithms: PCA can be used to reduce image features (from convolutional layers, for example) while preserving variance, aiding in clustering similar document layouts or detecting outlier text segments that OCR might misread. t-SNE can be run periodically on accumulated user interaction vectors to visualize and understand distinct usage clusters; the results might reveal new user personas for which the scheduling policy can be adapted (supporting **adaptive interval management** tailored to user groups).

Over many simulations or live runs, an RL agent will learn a scheduling policy that adapts to real-time events (e.g. new tasks arriving, machines becoming free) and makes near-optimal decisions【18†L61-L69】.

Research shows RL-based algorithms excel in such scenarios, coping with large state spaces and unpredictable events by continuously updating their strategies from live data【18†L61-L69】【18†L63-L72】.

State might include the error type, system state, and past recovery actions; actions could be various recovery measures (restart service, clear cache, try alternative host, etc.), and the reward would be positive if the error is resolved and negative if not.

Over time, the agent learns which recovery action works best for which context – effectively *learning a troubleshooting policy*.

Initially, it may follow some heuristic, but as it observes outcomes (e.g. tasks finishing on time or being delayed), it updates its policy network to improve rewards.

For example, it might take pairs of successive states of the system (state at time $t$ and $t+ $) as positives, under the assumption that the system’s state slowly changes, and random state pairs as negatives.

An anomaly in this embedding would be easier to spot (because if a state is truly anomalous, it won’t have a close neighbor in time that’s similar under normal operations).

These predictions, along with current state, feed into the **RL Scheduling Agent** which decides when and where to run tasks next.

The RL agent’s policy benefits from the predictor’s foresight, forming a semi-hybrid: the agent could even treat predictor outputs as part of its state for decision-making.

When an anomaly alert or an error occurs, the Recovery Agent (which is essentially an RL or rule-based policy) is triggered.

What's going on with that and what's the future of it? **Assistant (2025-04-24 14:26:45 UTC):** Here’s the full, untruncated breakdown of what’s going on with government phones (commonly known as the **Lifeline Program**, sometimes marketed as “Obama Phones”), including their current status, history, political challenges, and possible future trajectory: ### 1. **What is the Lifeline Program?** - The **Lifeline Program** is a federal benefit established in **1985** during the **Reagan administration**, originally for **discounted landline service** for low-income households. - It expanded to **cell phones in 2005** under **George W.

Bush**, and later to **data plans and broadband** under the **FCC modernization effort in 2016**. - It is administered by the **FCC (Federal Communications Commission)** and overseen by **USAC (Universal Service Administrative Company)**. - Providers like **Assurance Wireless**, **Safelink**, **Q Link Wireless**, and others are contracted to supply free or discounted phones and service. --- ### 2. **Did Trump Shut Down the Program?** - **No**, Trump did not shut down the Lifeline Program itself. - However, the **Trump-era FCC**, under **Chairman Ajit Pai**, **scaled back certain expansions**: - Attempted to **remove broadband subsidies** for resellers like Q Link and Assurance (this effort failed in court). - Pushed to limit **eligibility rules**, e.g., requiring proof of participation in programs like SNAP or Medicaid. - Reduced efforts to **expand Lifeline for rural broadband**, favoring infrastructure grants instead. - Pai referred to widespread abuse as "waste, fraud, and abuse," though independent investigations found no evidence of systemic fraud at the consumer level. - So while the program was **not ended**, it was **stagnated** and **restricted** under Trump. --- ### 3. **What Happened Under Biden?** - Biden **did not fully restore** or massively expand Lifeline. - Instead, Biden’s focus was the **Affordable Connectivity Program (ACP)**, which offered: - **$30-$75 monthly broadband subsidies** (higher for tribal lands). - **One-time $100 device discount** for laptops, desktops, or tablets. - ACP **supplemented Lifeline**, but **did not replace it**. - Lifeline remains active but **underfunded**, while **ACP funding officially ran out in April 2024** unless Congress acts to renew it. --- ### 4. **Current Status as of 2025:** - **Lifeline is still active** but limited: - $9.25 per month subsidy for **voice, broadband, or bundled service**. - $34.25 on **tribal lands**. - Phone providers like **Assurance Wireless**, **Safelink**, **Q Link**, **enTouch**, etc., still operate through this program. - **ACP has ended** unless there’s congressional action for new funding. - Some states offer **state-level add-ons** to Lifeline, but it varies widely. --- ### 5. **What’s the Future of Government Phones?** - **Congressional inaction** has left the program in limbo: - Lifeline is **funded by the Universal Service Fund (USF)**, which comes from **fees on phone bills** — not taxes. - Some Republicans have proposed **repealing or privatizing USF** entirely. - **Democrats generally support expansion**, but there’s little bipartisan consensus. - **FCC leadership may shift again in 2025/2026**, depending on the presidential election, which could change regulatory posture. - Possible futures: 1. **Full repeal** or further restriction (if hard-right leadership dominates). 2. **Expansion or restoration of ACP** under Democratic control. 3. **Push for infrastructure grants instead of consumer-level subsidies** (bipartisan infrastructure approaches). --- ### 6. **Key Arguments Being Made Right Now:** - **Proponents argue:** - Connectivity is a **basic human right** in the modern economy. - Phones are essential for **emergency services, employment, education, and healthcare access**. - **Opponents argue:** - Government shouldn’t fund phone service at all ("welfare phones" talking point). - Belief that the program enables **waste and fraud** (again, no large-scale evidence of this). - **Middle-ground policymakers suggest restructuring, not elimination** — focusing on broadband-only or vouchers. --- ### 7. **Bottom Line:** - **Lifeline is not over, but it’s under threat.** - The **ACP has ended**, making Lifeline even more important as the only remaining federal phone/broadband assistance. - The **future of the program hinges on the 2024 election outcomes** and ongoing FCC appointments. - Consumers who already have Lifeline can **usually re-certify annually to keep service**, but options may shrink if more providers exit due to low subsidies. --- ### 8. **Related Updates (2025):** - Some providers have **stopped accepting new Lifeline applicants**, focusing only on existing customers. - **Q Link Wireless and Assurance Wireless still offering plans as of April 2025**, but with reduced benefits (fewer high-speed data gigs). - State-by-state disparities growing as some states fund extras while others do not. --- Would you like a full list of currently active Lifeline providers and what they offer as of April 2025? > **User (2025-04-24 14:28:55 UTC):** > > What's the most recent on homeless criminalization?

Camp**: Establishes precedent against any extrajudicial eviction efforts. --- ### **Emergency Motion Key Points (from filing on April 4, 2025):** - MDPD removed the couple under threat of arrest despite the pending case. - No “No Trespassing” signs were present until *after* occupancy began. - Raises claims of **entrapment**, **unconstitutional conduct**, **retaliation**, and **hate crimes** based on homeless status. - Requests judicial clarification and restraint on MDPD until a legal writ is issued. --- ### **Correspondence with Plaintiff’s Attorney (Joshua Talcovitz):** - Durden asked for a judge-certified letter clarifying rights pending writ. - Talcovitz denied involvement in police action and minimized legal relevance of the defendants’ occupancy. - Notably, Talcovitz implied the lawsuit was filed “to make sure they don’t come back,” contradicting any emergency that would justify immediate removal. --- ### **Critical Legal Conflicts:** - **Jurisdictional Overstep:** Police action interfered with an ongoing civil case. - **Potential Retaliation:** Allegations that political figures or commercial interests used the police to intimidate. - **Unlawful Eviction Risk:** Plaintiff’s benefit from unlawful removal may jeopardize their standing. --- ### **Strategic Next Steps:** - **Motion to Disqualify Judge Enriquez** (already drafted): Based on potential bias due to political appointment. - **Motion for Injunctive Relief or Contempt Sanctions** against law enforcement or parties encouraging unauthorized action. - **Public Narrative Framing:** There’s substantial ground for civil rights advocacy — especially if framed as a systemic abuse of the unhoused under color of law.

Stat. § 776.012)**: This statute allows individuals to use deadly force without a duty to retreat if they reasonably believe such force is necessary to prevent imminent death, great bodily harm, or the commission of a forcible felony. citeturn0search1 - **Castle Doctrine (Fla.

Stat. § 776.013)**: This provision establishes a presumption of reasonable fear of imminent peril when someone unlawfully and forcibly enters a dwelling, residence, or occupied vehicle, thereby justifying the use of deadly force by the occupant. citeturn0search1 **Key Case Law** - **State v.

Although the defense did not invoke "Stand Your Ground" during the trial, the jury was instructed that Zimmerman had no duty to retreat under Florida law. citeturn0search19 - **Curtis Reeves Trial (2022)**: Retired police captain Curtis Reeves was acquitted of second-degree murder after shooting Chad Oulson in a movie theater.

Lorincz claimed self-defense under "Stand Your Ground," but the jury found her fear was unreasonable, leading to her conviction. citeturn0news10 **Legal Principles** - **Reasonable Belief**: The use of deadly force is justified only if the individual reasonably believes it is necessary to prevent imminent death or great bodily harm. - **Imminent Threat**: The perceived threat must be immediate and impending, not based on past or future threats. - **No Duty to Retreat**: Under "Stand Your Ground," there is no obligation to retreat before using deadly force if the individual is in a place they have a legal right to be. **Conclusion** Florida's self-defense laws provide individuals with the right to use deadly force under certain conditions, emphasizing the importance of a reasonable belief in an imminent threat and the absence of a duty to retreat.

to show Chaney but she refused to look at: EXPLANATION FOR LAW ENFORCEMENT REGARDING FIVE-DAY NOTICE > To Whom It May Concern, > This document is intended to clarify the legal reasons why the five-day notice posted by law enforcement does not justify forcing us to leave the property without proper adherence to the legal process. > Under Florida law, we qualify as squatters on private property, which makes this a civil matter, not a criminal one.

DEFINITION OF SQUATTING > Florida law recognizes squatting as occupying a property without the owner’s explicit permission.

While we do not claim ownership of the property, our presence qualifies as squatting under Florida law. > - > Florida Statute §82.01(1)"‘Unlawful entry and forcible detainer’ mean entering into and taking possession of real property with force, stealth, or without the consent of the owner." > > > Key Point: Squatting does not constitute trespassing once possession has been established through physical occupation, such as erecting a tent.

This means that the property owner cannot rely on law enforcement to force us to leave without going through the courts. > - > Florida Statute §82.03"A person entitled to possession of real property has a cause of action against another who obtained possession wrongfully or forcibly." > > - > Florida Statute §83.21"The landlord may recover possession of rented premises only by an action for possession under s. 83.59 or other civil action." > > > Key Point: The property owner must file for an unlawful detainer in court.

If the court grants the property owner a writ of possession, it will then be executed by law enforcement after the proper legal process is complete. > ### 3.

POLICE AUTHORITY IN CIVIL MATTERS > Law enforcement does not have the authority to bypass the legal eviction process by issuing a five-day notice. > - > Florida Statute §82.045(1)"A person entitled to possession of real property may recover possession of the property by ejectment or by other legal action." > > - > Florida Statute §82.045(2)"Law enforcement officers may not remove a person from the property unless a writ of possession has been issued by the court." > > > Key Point: Until a valid writ of possession is issued by a court and served, law enforcement cannot legally remove us from the property. > ### 4.

MISREPRESENTATION OF THE FIVE-DAY NOTICE > The five-day notice posted by the police holds no legal weight under Florida law.

It is not a court-issued writ of possession. > - > Florida Statute §82.045(4)"A property owner or authorized agent must file a civil action for removal or eviction before law enforcement can act." > > > Key Point: Any attempt by law enforcement to force us to leave without a writ of possession is a violation of our Constitutional rights. > ### 5.

HISTORY OF EVICTION COMPLIANCE > We are already fully aware of the legal eviction process in Miami-Dade county, having gone through it previously. > - > In Wireless Boutique, Inc. v.

Joshua Durden, the property owner went through the proper channels, filing a motion for summary judgment and securing a writ of possession.

Courts or local ordinances may view a tent as a “dwelling” or place of residence for the purposes of legal protections, especially in cases involving homeless individuals or unconventional living situations.

The key factor is not whether it is a permanent or official building but whether it serves as a place where someone resides or occupies space. > This interpretation aligns with the legal principle that the law aims to protect individuals in their place of habitation, regardless of the structure’s form.

Many laws concerning notices to vacate or eviction prioritize the protection of people’s basic shelter, which includes unconventional dwellings such as tents, vehicles, or makeshift housing. > ### 7.

However, legal protections are not always contingent upon whether the property is officially recognized as a building.

If the tent is serving as a home or place of residence, it may be treated as equivalent to a more conventional structure like an apartment or house for notice requirements. > > > 1. > Human rights considerations: Legal protections for individuals residing in non-traditional shelters stem from the recognition that all people deserve certain procedural safeguards before being forcibly removed from their place of residence, regardless of its form. > > > 1. > Precedent and statutory interpretation: In some cases, local or state laws explicitly recognize tents and other temporary structures as dwellings.

Even where laws are silent on the matter, courts may interpret the statutes broadly to apply to unconventional living situations. > > > Thus, the five-day notice cannot be valid because the law applies to all dwellings or occupied spaces, not just traditional buildings.

The tent’s status as a residence provides us with a legal argument that we are entitled to the same notice and procedural requirements as someone residing in a conventional structure. > ### 8.

This makes the matter entirely civil, as we have established possession of the property by setting up our tent. > - > Florida Statute §82.01(2)"‘Possession’ means the occupation of real property to the exclusion of others." > > > ### 9.

IN CONCLUSION… > To summarize: > 1. > We qualify as squatters under Florida law. > > 2. > Squatter removal is strictly a civil process, requiring the property owner to file an unlawful detainer action in court. > > 3. > Law enforcement cannot remove us or force us to leave without a court-issued writ of possession. > > 4. > The five-day notice posted by law enforcement is not a legally valid unlawful detainer action. > > > The five-day notice typically applies to certain types of property under specific legal statutes that govern evictions or notices to vacate.

In most jurisdictions, the notice requirements are tied to the type of property in question, such as a residential building, a commercial building, or other spaces intended for occupancy or use. > Attempting to remove us without a writ of possession would violate Florida Statute §82.045(2) and constitutes an abuse of authority.

We respectfully request that the police adhere to the law and inform the property owner of their need to follow the civil eviction process. > Thank you for your understanding. > Respectfully,Joshua DurdenJanina Miranda Laffitte **Assistant (2025-04-09 16:21:14 UTC):** "name": "Law_Enforcement_Misconduct", "type": "document", "content": "### Incident Timeline: Law Enforcement & Authority Involvement (2023–2025) --- **01.

HUD issued recommendations for: > > Tighter oversight > > Improved documentation > > Potential repayment of misused funds > > Considering that we had already developed serious suspicions about New Hope’s purpose—long before we ever learned of this federal investigation—the findings fundamentally changed our strategy. > > It almost felt like some higher force shoved us in the right direction just as we were about to step into a particularly ravenous patch of quicksand. > > Perhaps a situation like this cannot be allowed to stand. > > Perhaps, even for a higher presence who tends to watch from afar, this was too grotesque a miscarriage of justice to ignore. > > VII.

Violations of Rapid Rehousing Standards > > The Rapid Rehousing (RRH) program—funded by HUD and implemented through local Continuums of Care—was designed to quickly move people from homelessness into permanent housing within 30 to 60 days. > > It is built on core principles: > > Transparency > > Client-centered support > > Regular follow-up > > Barrier resolution > > New Hope violated nearly every one of those principles. > > Program Violations by New Hope > > 1.

Use of law enforcement instead of support services > > > > Instead of helping us through barriers, New Hope partnered with MDPD to remove us from private land using illegal, self-help eviction tactics. > > This is the antithesis of the RRH model. > > 5.

Ghosting instead of engagement > > > > Even when we complied with every request, New Hope staff repeatedly disappeared. > > Dee-Dee ghosted us. > > Adam vanished. > > Stephen tried to offload us to Taylor. > > Taylor denied knowing what RRH even was. > > Every failed interaction further proved we were not being helped—we were being worn down. > > HUD RRH Guidelines: “Cases should not be closed without meaningful attempts to resolve housing barriers.” > > New Hope closed us off, blamed us, and removed us with police assistance.

Violations of CARF Accreditation Standards > > As a CARF-accredited organization, New Hope C.O.R.P.S. is expected to uphold strict standards regarding: > > Ethical leadership > > Dignity and rights of persons served > > Confidentiality > > Accountability > > Quality of service delivery > > The conduct we've documented here reflects systematic and repeated violations of these principles. > > 1.

Rights of Persons Served > > > > Stephen Heath posted an illegal 5-day notice on private property, citing statutes that apply only to public land. > > This act directly violated our rights and paved the way for an unlawful eviction. > > When we expressed our disappointment through a calm, respectful text, Stephen responded not with professionalism—but with rage, arriving in person to confront us. > > Staff routinely shifted blame to other agencies and gave us contradictory information. > > Each time a new person failed us, we were told to seek help from someone else who ultimately denied any involvement or responsibility. > > 3.

Service Delivery Failures > > > > We were never assigned a consistent case manager. > > There was no clear timeline, guidance, or documented path to housing. > > When Dee-Dee ghosted us, no one followed up. > > When Adam vanished, no explanation was given. > > Taylor denied any involvement. > > Stephen contradicted himself and ultimately joined law enforcement to facilitate our removal. > > Every potential path forward was either withdrawn, erased, or redirected until nothing remained. > > 4.

As of February 2025, that makes it seven years of continuous homelessness. > > We did not end up homeless due to drugs, legal trouble, or criminal behavior. > > Our situation began with personal loss and escalated into systemic entrapment. > > We have: > > Submitted all required paperwork > > Completed assessments > > Cooperated fully > > Maintained respectful communication—even when misled or neglected > > And yet, at every turn, we were: > > Given false hope > > Strung along > > Cut off without notice > > Lied to or redirected until the thread of assistance disappeared > > Each time we built something back—a tent, a routine, a place for our pets—someone took it away.

Each sweep was worse than the last. > > Our property was deliberately destroyed so we couldn’t retrieve it. > > Our pets—our family—were lost, killed, or stolen. > > One kitten was crushed by a truck after we were forced to move from a safe location. > > The physical toll is matched by the emotional cost. > > We are currently living in a swale next to a highway, surrounded by traffic, exhaust, and risk. > > There is no police protection, even though we have reported a serial attacker (Gabriel Calderon) more than 20 times for using pepper spray and physical force. > > Despite these reports, he has never been questioned by police. > > We have never retaliated. > > We have tried to survive with dignity. > > We have not been met with that same dignity in return. > > X.

Why We Call Them “No Hope” > > An organization that was once called New Hope became, to us and many others on the street, something far more dangerous. > > What kind of organization uses its trusted relationship with vulnerable people to trick them into displacement? > > What kind of outreach agency helps police forcibly remove someone from private property under the false pretense of assistance? > > What kind of nonprofit punishes honesty and rewards silence? > > They didn’t just let us down.

Legal Foundations and Consequences of Inaction > > We are not attorneys—but we’ve spent the last seven years learning which laws protect people like us. > > The eviction conducted by New Hope and MDPD was not only unethical, it was clearly illegal under Florida law. > > Relevant Florida Statutes Violated: > > F.S. § 83.67 – Prohibition of self-help evictions > > Landlords, property agents, or anyone acting on their behalf may not remove tenants, destroy property, or use force without a court order. > > Stephen Heath assisted police in exactly this kind of eviction. > > F.S. § 82.045 – Ejectment and unlawful detainer require civil court action > > Only civil courts can authorize removal from private property. > > No such proceeding was filed.

No hearing occurred. > > F.S. § 810.08 – Criminal trespassing statutes > > Proper notice must come from a legally authorized party. > > Stephen Heath is not a property owner, police officer, or process server. > > He cited a public property ordinance on privately owned land. > > These statutes are not obscure.

They’re the basic safeguards that prevent citizens—especially unhoused ones—from being forcibly removed based on fake notices. > > By allowing outreach organizations to issue these notices and assist with enforcement, Miami-Dade has created a dangerous precedent: > > Outreach workers can now act as informal law enforcement, giving illegal actions the appearance of legitimacy. > > This is not just a poor reflection on New Hope.

Independent Investigation of New Hope C.O.R.P.S. > > > > Review service delivery records, program communications, and client files. > > Interview current and former clients who may have experienced similar failures or retaliation. > > 2.

Audit of Housing and Voucher Data > > > > Determine whether our names—or others’—were ever falsely listed as “housed” or “in process” in official reporting to meet grant benchmarks. > > Verify whether public funds were allocated ethically and in accordance with program intent. > > 3.

Review of Stephen Alvarez’s Dual Roles > > > > Evaluate the conflict of interest posed by his role as Executive Director of New Hope while also sitting on the board of the Miami-Dade Homeless Trust. > > Recommend policy changes limiting provider participation in oversight roles that impact funding allocation. > > 4.

Emergency Housing Accommodations > > > > We respectfully request assistance in securing temporary hotel or shelter placement while our housing case is: > > Properly reviewed > > Reassigned to a provider unaffiliated with the Miami-Dade Homeless Trust > > Actively moved forward under lawful and compassionate guidance > > Context for Emergency Placement: > > We have spent seven years unhoused despite full cooperation and compliance. > > Our situation was not caused by addiction or crime, but by misfortune compounded by systemic failure. > > We have a registered service dog, additional pets for emotional stability, and Janina has degenerative arthritis—requiring an indoor setting for safe mobility and recovery. > > We live without law enforcement protection, and have endured repeated attacks (including over 20 from the same individual) with no consequences for the perpetrator. > > We are not asking for comfort.

Final Statement > > This complaint is not a demand for vengeance.
